# OpenEvidence : cervical, épaule, coude et poignet (CERV-P, EPAU-F, EPAU-P, COUD-F, COUD-P, POIG-P)

Document Word du Dr Morand « The_accuracy_of_each_finding_depends_on_the_study_population_and_reference_standard.docx », converti en Markdown (`pandoc -t gfm`) le 24 novembre 2026.

The accuracy of each finding depends on the study population and reference standard: Kanavel-sign studies for flexor sheath infection, crystal-proven cohorts for gout and CPPD, early-arthritis cohorts for rheumatoid and psoriatic arthritis, and electrodiagnostic or imaging standards for cervical radiculopathy. Let me check the diagnostic-accuracy literature for each of the four groups.  
  
  
  
**None of the ten provisional pairs is confirmed as it stands.** Six codes have published accuracy data, complete for four (P35, P36, P40, P42) and partial for two (P34, P39). One has odds ratios only (P38), and three have no data (P33, P37, P41). No meta-analysis pools any of these findings individually, and only the gout data come from primary care.  
  
† marks likelihood ratios (and confidence intervals) calculated from the published counts or percentages; the authors did not report them.

**How each provisional value compares**

**Pyogenic flexor tenosynovitis**  
  
- **P33 (LR+ 6, LR− 0.5): unsupported.** No controlled study tested wound history as a diagnostic finding. In one series of 66 confirmed cases, 32% followed trauma, 27% an animal bite, and 35% had no identified cause.[<u>\[2\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00402-024-05269-7)  
  
- **P34 (LR+ 5, LR− 0.3): both values fall outside the published range.** The only controlled study reports the four Kanavel signs as a range, so sheath tenderness cannot have an LR+ above about 3.2 or an LR− above about 0.17 in that cohort. Sheath tenderness was an independent predictor on multivariable analysis. The exact per-sign figures require the full-text tables of Kennedy 2017 or Siska 2022 (PMID 35261842).<u>[\[1\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28720000)[\[15\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35261842)</u>  
  
**Rheumatoid arthritis**  
  
- **P35 (LR+ 6, LR− 0.2): not confirmed, and the available figure does not transfer.** Every patient in the Saraux cohort already had arthritis, which depresses specificity. In a general hand-pain population specificity would likely be higher, but no primary-care accuracy study was found.<u>[\[3\]](https://doi.org/10.1002/1529-0131(200111)44:11%3C2485::aid-art428%3E3.0.co;2-s?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)[\[16\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30798292)</u>  
  
- The Banal meta-analysis (PMID 18728049) pools only the full 1987 criteria set, not individual items.[<u>\[17\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18728049)  
  
**Gout and calcium pyrophosphate arthritis**  
  
- **P36 (LR+ 6, LR− 1): correct both.** In primary care a self-reported history of gout shifts probability modestly in both directions. The cohort contained only patients the physician already suspected of gout, and the first metatarsophalangeal joint dominated; none of these data are specific to the wrist or hand.[<u>\[4\]</u>](https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jamainternalmedicine/fullarticle/10.1001/archinternmed.2010.196?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)  
  
- **P37 (LR+ 6, LR− 1): no data.** The 2023 ACR/EULAR classification criteria report performance only for the full criteria set.[<u>\[5\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1002/art.42619?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)  
  
- **P38 (LR+ 5, LR− 0.3): unsupported.** Redness was not an independent predictor in primary care (adjusted OR 1.57, 95% CI 0.73–3.39). In the SUGAR study erythema had a multivariable OR of 2.13 (95% CI 1.06–4.29). Item-level counts exist in the full-text tables of both studies but were not retrievable. No data were found for calcium pyrophosphate arthritis.<u>[\[4\]](https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jamainternalmedicine/fullarticle/10.1001/archinternmed.2010.196?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)[\[7\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27112094)</u>  
  
- **P39 (LR+ 15, LR− 0.8): direction supported, value not.** Tophus coincided completely with crystal-proven gout in primary care, so no finite LR+ can be derived. The SUGAR multivariable OR was 7.29 (95% CI 2.42–21.99). Sensitivity, and therefore LR−, was not retrievable. The ACP systematic review reports accuracy for algorithms only, not single findings.<u>[\[4\]](https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jamainternalmedicine/fullarticle/10.1001/archinternmed.2010.196?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)[\[7\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27112094)[\[18\]](https://doi.org/10.7326/m16-0462)</u>  
  
**Psoriatic arthritis**  
  
- **P40 (LR+ 8, LR− 0.9): the LR− looks wrong for an inflammatory-arthritis population.** Absence of psoriasis argued strongly against psoriatic arthritis in the Coates study.[<u>\[8\]</u>](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/art.34536?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)  
  
- These figures are likely inflated: the design was case-control, and psoriasis itself shapes the rheumatologist's diagnosis.[<u>\[8\]</u>](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/art.34536?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)  
  
- Among patients with psoriasis and joint symptoms, only 56% have psoriatic arthritis, so the LR+ will be lower in an unselected hand-pain population.[<u>\[19\]</u>](https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jama/fullarticle/10.1001/jama.2020.4006?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)  
  
- The per-item results of the original CASPAR derivation study were not retrievable.[<u>\[20\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1002/art.21972?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)  
  
**Cervical radiculopathy**  
  
- **P41 (LR+ 6, LR− 0.5): no data.** The one study of interview items (Sleijser-Koehorst, Physiotherapy, 2021, PMID 33309074) found that no history item reached an LR+ of 5 or an LR− of 0.20. Wainner 2003 (PMID 12544957) included self-report items, but their values were not retrievable.<u>[\[9\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33309074)[\[21\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12544957)</u>  
  
- **P42 (LR+ 5, LR− 0.4): within the published range, but that range is too wide to confirm a single value.** Two systematic reviews declined to pool because test technique differed between studies, and graded the likelihood ratios as very low certainty.[<u>\[11\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12891-026-09551-0)  
  
- The highest values come from surgical cohorts with high prevalence; the electrodiagnostic study gives the lowest sensitivity.<u>[\[13\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11805661)[\[14\]](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00586-017-5153-0)</u>  
  
- The APTA 2017 neck pain guideline cites sensitivity 0.50 and specificity 0.86–0.93.[<u>\[22\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28666405)  
  
- All included studies were in secondary care.[<u>\[11\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12891-026-09551-0)

**Items to verify from full text**

\- Per-sign 2×2 counts for sheath tenderness in Kennedy 2017 and Siska 2022.  
  
- Redness and tophus counts in Janssens 2010 Table 1 and Taylor 2015 Table 2.  
  
- The pooled likelihood ratio for tophi in the EULAR 2006 gout evidence review (Zhang, Ann Rheum Dis), which was not retrievable here.  
  
- Spurling and self-report item values in Wainner 2003 Table 3 and Sleijser-Koehorst 2021.  
  
- PMIDs for Saraux 2001, Coates 2012, Janssens 2010 and Taylor 2015.  
  
Would you like the same extraction for the remaining Kanavel signs and the other cervical provocation tests (upper limb neurodynamic tests, shoulder abduction relief, distraction), where pooled estimates do exist?

[<u>Sensitivity and specificity of the Classification of Psoriatic Arthritis criteria in early psoriatic arthritis</u>](https://doi.org/10.1002/art.34536?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral). Coates LC, Conaghan PG, Emery P, et al. Arthritis and Rheumatism. 2012;64(10):3150-5. doi:10.1002/art.34536.

Each row depends on whether a published study reports sensitivity and specificity for that exact finding against a defined reference standard, as opposed to a related provocative test or a composite decision rule. Let me check the diagnostic accuracy literature for each diagnosis group.  
  
  
  
**Only one of the 25 codes (POIG-P20) has a published sensitivity and specificity pair for the coded finding itself.** Two more (P16, P17) have case-series sensitivity only, so no likelihood ratio can be calculated. For the other 22, no accuracy study of the finding was found, so those provisional values can be neither confirmed nor corrected.

**Accuracy by code**

LRs for P20 are calculated from the published sensitivity and specificity; no confidence interval is available without the 2×2 counts.

**What the one usable row changes**

For POIG-P20, the published LR+ of about 4.9 matches the current value of 4, but the published LR− of about 0.07 is lower than the current 0.2.[<u>\[14\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25986651) Patients were recruited by diagnostic group, which tends to inflate accuracy, so these values are likely optimistic for primary care.[<u>\[14\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25986651)

**Why most rows are empty**

\- **Fractures (P8, P9, P23, P24):** Deformity and radial tenderness are reported as odds ratios inside multivariable decision rules, not as stand-alone accuracy. A systematic review of metacarpal and phalangeal fractures found one physical examination accuracy study and none from general practice.<u>[\[1\]](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12891-015-0829-2)[\[2\]](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00068-020-01474-2)[\[16\]](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12891-019-2988-z)</u>  
  
- **Tendon and ligament injuries (P14, P25, P28–P31):** A systematic review of non-chronic finger, hand and wrist tendon and ligament injuries found no study of history taking and only limited evidence for examination.[<u>\[7\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33154046)  
  
- **Trigger finger and Dupuytren disease (P21, P22, P26, P27):** The clinical findings are the case definition, so there is no independent reference standard.<u>[\[15\]](https://www.cochranelibrary.com/cdsr/doi/10.1002/14651858.CD005617.pub2/full?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)[\[20\]](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12891-019-2606-0)</u>  
  
- **De Quervain tenosynovitis (P12, P13):** Radial styloid pain and tenderness were entry criteria in the main accuracy study, so they could not be evaluated as tests.[<u>\[5\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1177/1753193412475043)

**Nearest published evidence (not the coded finding)**

These are related tests or composite rules, listed so the gap is visible; they are not substitutes for the coded items.

**Provisional values that sit uneasily with indirect evidence**

No replacement values are offered here, only reasons for caution.  
  
- **P8:** Visible deformity is often minimal in buckle fractures, which argues against a single LR− covering both displaced and buckle fractures.[<u>\[33\]</u>](https://www.aafp.org/pubs/afp/issues/2021/0315/p345.html)  
  
- **P14:** In 908 arthroscopy patients the fovea sign had 44% specificity for any TFCC lesion, so pain location alone is unlikely to be strongly discriminating.[<u>\[30\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00402-016-2441-9)  
  
- **P17:** Ring and little finger numbness does not localise an ulnar lesion to the wrist rather than the elbow.[<u>\[34\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1007/s12178-016-9327-x)  
  
- **P23:** Up to 90% of uninjured people have fingernail overlap of up to 50%, which undermines the specificity of apparent scissoring.[<u>\[35\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1007/s12178-017-9380-0)  
  
- **P26:** Pain is uncommon in Dupuytren disease, so palm pain location is a weak anchor for this diagnosis.[<u>\[21\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1177/17531934261463402)  
  
- **P30, P31:** In cadavers an isolated A2 or A4 rupture produced no detectable bowstringing, so clinical signs of pulley rupture may be insensitive.[<u>\[36\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9698006)  
  
- **P32:** Clenched-fist bite injuries are characterised by joint penetration (95% at the MCP joint), not flexor sheath infection.[<u>\[27\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1177/1753193415576249)

**Limits of this search**

Full-text per-finding tables were not accessible for several studies and could fill some rows. Eyler 2018 (PMID 28912085) calculated sensitivity and specificity for 18 wrist findings, including deformity, against any wrist fracture.[<u>\[37\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28912085) Kennedy 2017 (PMID 28720000) has per-sign Kanavel data, and the Rubin 1996 cadaveric Elson study could not be retrieved.<u>[\[32\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28720000)[\[19\]](https://doi.org/10.1177/17531934231183259)</u>  
  
Would you like a closer look at which validated provocative tests (for example the thumb adduction and extension tests or the ulnar fovea sign) could replace the unvalidated items in the questionnaire?

The accuracy of each finding depends on which studies measured it against a defined reference standard and in what setting, so the open question is whether each sign has a meta-analysis or only single-centre data; let me check the diagnostic accuracy literature for each of the seven signs.  
  
  
  
**None of the seven current LR pairs is confirmed by a published study.** Three codes (P1, P2, P6) have no published LR for the target diagnosis, and four (P3, P4, P5, P7) have published values that differ from the current ones. No meta-analysis was found except for scaphoid fracture (P3).  
  
Values marked † are not printed in the source; they are arithmetic from its published sensitivity/specificity or 2×2 table.

**Verdict on each current value**

\- **POIG-P1 (LR+ 5, LR− 0.8): unverified.** Only the specificity was retrieved, and it applies to any adult wrist fracture, so no LR can be formed. A Dutch multicentre study reported hematoma only as an odds ratio of 2.76 (1.95–3.90) for any wrist fracture. No pediatric or buckle-fracture data were found.<u>[\[1\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28912085)[\[16\]](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00068-020-01474-2)</u>  
  
- **POIG-P2 (LR+ 1.5, LR− 0.1): unverified, and the LR− is probably too optimistic.** High sensitivity is supported, but neither study's retrieved text gives specificity. The Turkmen figures (sensitivity 89.7%, NPV 81.3%, 29 of 119 with fracture) imply about 13 true negatives among 90 non-fractures. That back-calculation gives specificity near 14%, LR+ near 1.05 and LR− near 0.72; it needs checking against the full-text 2×2 table. The Amsterdam Wrist Rules distal radius model did not retain painful dorsiflexion.<u>[\[1\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28912085)[\[2\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26113244)[\[17\]](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12891-015-0829-2)</u>  
  
- **POIG-P3 (LR+ 10, LR− 0.2): correct by splitting the item.**  
  
- Resisted supination is the only component with a rule-in signal, but the two estimates (6.1 and 45.0) are far apart and rest on very few studies.<u>[\[5\]](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119616870.ch16)[\[6\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37169546)</u>  
  
- Resisted pronation is uninformative.[<u>\[5\]</u>](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119616870.ch16)  
  
- The Carpenter per-finding LRs were taken from a later textbook table that summarises the meta-analysis, and the confidence limits should be checked in the original paper.[<u>\[5\]</u>](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/9781119616870.ch16)  
  
- Pain on forearm rotation is not scaphoid-specific: it also predicted any wrist fracture and distal radius fracture.<u>[\[1\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28912085)[\[17\]](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12891-015-0829-2)</u>  
  
- **POIG-P4 (LR+ 5, LR− 0.3): unsupported.** The manoeuvre usually taught as "Finkelstein" is the Eichhoff test, which was close to uninformative in the only ultrasound-referenced study. That study's specificity rests on 7 patients. For the true Finkelstein test, a JAMA review calls sensitivity unclear and cites 100% specificity in 72 wrists; the primary study was not retrieved.<u>[\[7\]](https://doi.org/10.1177/1753193412475043)[\[8\]](https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jama/fullarticle/10.1001/jama.2022.8481?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)</u>  
  
- **POIG-P5 (LR+ 3, LR− 0.15): matches no single source.** The original description implies strong accuracy for foveal or ulnotriquetral ligament injury, but it is retrospective and single-surgeon. The largest arthroscopy cohort shows weak accuracy for any TFCC lesion. In a later series of 73 sign-positive wrists, only 21 had foveal tears.<u>[\[9\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17398352)[\[10\]](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00402-016-2441-9)[\[18\]](https://doi.org/10.1177/17531934241279921)</u>  
  
- **POIG-P6 (LR+ 8, LR− 0.2): unverified.** Published data are associations, not accuracy.  
  
- Heberden nodes carried an odds ratio of 5.15 (4.37–6.08) for osteophyte in the same digit.[<u>\[12\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15677702)  
  
- Agreement between Heberden nodes and DIP osteophytes was poor (kappa 0.36).[<u>\[13\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9709182)  
  
- Clinical signs overall (hand and knee, not nodes alone) had sensitivity 20–49% and specificity 87–99% against radiographs in 541 women. If nodes behave similarly, an LR− of 0.2 is not attainable.[<u>\[19\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1877852)  
  
- The ACR 1990 criteria include hard-tissue enlargement, so accuracy against that standard is inflated by incorporation.[<u>\[20\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2242058)  
  
- **POIG-P7 (LR+ 10, LR− 0.3): corrected for single signs.** Individual signs rule out better than they rule in. No study gives the accuracy of all four signs together. In a pediatric surgical series all four were present in only 34% and at least three in 62%. One review states there is no good research on the sensitivity or specificity of Kanavel signs.<u>[\[14\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28720000)[\[21\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28341068)[\[22\]](https://doi.org/10.1007/s15010-018-1236-z)</u>

**Limits of this check**

Several figures come from abstracts or secondary tables. Full-text review of Eyler 2018, Turkmen 2015, Kennedy 2017 and Cicuttini 1998 would supply the missing sensitivity/specificity pairs. All retrieved data are from emergency or hand-surgery settings; no primary-care or sports-medicine study was found for any code.  
  
Would you like the same verification applied to the examination items that the clinical decision rules did retain, such as distal radius tenderness, swelling, and visible deformity?

Slaar A, Walenkamp MM, Bentohami A, et al. Pediatric Radiology. 2016;46(1):50-60. doi:10.1007/s00247-015-3436-3.

The answer hinges on whether consultation-based denominators (primary-care or sports-clinic cohorts of hand/wrist complaints) exist for each diagnosis, as opposed to population incidence figures, which cannot be converted into proportions. Let me check the primary-care cohort data, the trauma series, the sports-clinic series and the inflammatory and rare conditions in parallel.  
  
  
  
**None of the 29 age-specific estimates can be confirmed.** Only one primary-care cohort reports diagnoses as a share of hand/wrist consulters: the Dutch general practice cohort of Spies-Dorgelo (n=267, mean age 49.3, 74.2% women).[<u>\[1\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1002/art.24019?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral) It excluded patients under 18 and all acute injuries, and it lumps several of the listed categories together.[<u>\[1\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1002/art.24019?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral) No source stratifies proportions by 12–17, 18–39, 40–59 and 60+, so "by age band: no data" applies to every row.  
  
In the table, a figure labelled "other denominator" is not the requested proportion and should not be substituted for it. Sex ratios are population or case-series ratios, not multipliers on the share among consulters. "PMID n/r" means the PMID was not retrieved, not that none exists.

**Where the current estimates conflict with available data**

These comparisons use the adult, non-traumatic GP cohort or population sex ratios, so they flag discordance rather than supply corrected values.  
  
- **Ganglion (F5):** 9.0% in the GP cohort against an estimate of 4% at 50 years.[<u>\[1\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1002/art.24019?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)  
  
- **Rheumatoid arthritis (F26):** 7.9% as a GP working diagnosis against an estimate of 1%; the diagnoses were not confirmed. Incident cases are 66.8% female, a ratio near 2, against an implied ratio of about 4.9.<u>[\[1\]](https://doi.org/10.1002/art.24019?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)[\[50\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30072114)</u>  
  
- **Lumped categories:** all tenosynovitis 15.8%, all osteoarthritis 16.9% and nerve entrapment 12.4% are the same order of magnitude as the summed estimates at 50 years, which is not a confirmation.[<u>\[1\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1002/art.24019?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)  
  
- **Perilunate dislocation (F4):** surgical series are 94.2% male (about 16:1), against an implied 4:1.[<u>\[16\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00068-023-02222-y)  
  
- **PIP dislocation (F19):** M:F is 3.1, against an implied 1.6.[<u>\[40\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1177/17531934251405707)  
  
- **Kienböck disease (F13):** M:F is 1.24 with a peak at 50–59 years, against an implied 2.1 and a peak at 30 years.[<u>\[30\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1177/17531934241312894)  
  
- **Distal radius fracture (F2):** the sex ratio reverses with age (61.3% male at 4–18 years, near 1 at ≤40, 4.0 female at ≥50), so a constant female multiplier does not fit.<u>[\[10\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36299238)[\[9\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25138260)</u>  
  
- **Scaphoid and hand fractures (F3, F18):** the male excess disappears or reverses after 60–65 years.<u>[\[12\]](https://doi.org/10.1177/1753193419849767)[\[38\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16945705)</u>  
  
- **Wrist osteoarthritis (F8):** the direction of the sex difference is inconsistent between sources.<u>[\[23\]](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00402-026-06423-z)[\[22\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21622766)</u>  
  
One structural point affects every sex multiplier. Women were 74.2% of non-traumatic consulters, so a population incidence ratio does not translate directly into a ratio of proportions among male and female consulters.[<u>\[1\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1002/art.24019?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)

**Main gaps**

\- The only consulter cohort excludes acute injury, so its denominator differs from "all wrist or hand pain", particularly in younger and athletic patients.[<u>\[1\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1002/art.24019?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)  
  
- No consultation-based diagnostic mix was found for ages 12–17, and none for general sports-medicine clinics; the climbers' series counts diagnoses rather than patients.[<u>\[2\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1177/03635465231199671)  
  
- The category "other" (15/267) in the GP cohort is unspecified, and the full text would be needed to see whether Dupuytren disease or trigger finger sit there.[<u>\[1\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1002/art.24019?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)  
  
Would it help to look for sport-specific injury surveillance datasets (high-school and collegiate) that could give an age-restricted diagnostic mix for the 12–22-year rows?

Three things decide this: whether tophus accuracy has been measured against crystal-proven gout, whether any study reports pain aggravated by neck movement as a history item, and what the pooled Spurling figures are. Let me check the diagnostic accuracy literature for each.  
  
  
  
Still open are the item-level figures: tophus frequency in crystal-positive versus crystal-negative patients, and the Wainner self-report and Spurling values against electrodiagnosis.  
  
  
  
**Only one of the three provisional pairs can be checked against published accuracy data, and that is the Spurling component of COUD‑P32.** No retrieved study reports sensitivity, specificity or likelihood ratios for a clinical tophus (P30) or for pain aggravated by neck movement (P31). No study addresses the elbow specifically for any of the three.

|--------------------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|

Closest: Taylor (SUGAR), Arthritis Care Res 2015, PMID not confirmed; reports an odds ratio only (see notes) | [<u>\[1\]</u>](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/acr.22585?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral), [<u>\[2\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27112094) |
| COUD‑P31 « Douleur aggravée par les mouvements du cou »            | no data                                                          | no data                                              | no data                                                      | no data                                    | —                                                                                                                                                                                              | —                                                  | No study found                                                                                                                                        | [<u>\[3\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33309074), [<u>\[4\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28644963)                                                          |
| COUD‑P32 « Spurling ou mouvements du cou reproduisant la douleur » | Spurling only. Review: 0.38–0.98 (not pooled). Tong: 0.30 (6/20) | Review: 0.84–1.00 (not pooled). Tong: 0.93 (160/172) | Review: not pooled, very low certainty. Tong: 4.3 (1.8–10.2) | Review: not pooled. Tong: 0.75 (0.56–1.01) | Review: 5 Spurling studies, all secondary care. Tong: 255 consecutive electrodiagnostic referrals for upper-limb nerve disorders; positive = pain or tingling radiating from shoulder to elbow | Review: imaging or surgery. Tong: electromyography | Thoomes, BMC Musculoskelet Disord 2026, PMID not retrieved; Tong, Spine 2002, PMID 11805661. Composite "or neck movements": no data                   | [<u>\[5\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12891-026-09551-0), [<u>\[6\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11805661)                                                        |

*Calculated here from the published counts (log method); the paper reports sensitivity and specificity only.*

**COUD‑P30: tophus (LR+ 8, LR− 0.9 not confirmed)**

\- **What exists:** SUGAR compared crystal-positive and crystal-negative patients in rheumatology clinics and reported a multivariable odds ratio of 7.29 (95% CI 2.42–21.99) for tophus. The univariate odds ratio was 10.0.<u>[\[2\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27112094)[\[1\]](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/acr.22585?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)</u>  
  
- **Why it cannot fill the row:** an odds ratio equals LR+ divided by LR−, so it cannot be split into the two without the 2×2 counts. The provisional pair implies an odds ratio of about 8.9, which is compatible with SUGAR but not confirmed by it.  
  
- **Bias:** SUGAR enrolled on a swollen joint or a subcutaneous nodule and accepted crystals from a tophus aspirate as the reference, so the tophus association is likely inflated.<u>[\[7\]](https://doi.org/10.1007/s11926-016-0594-8)[\[8\]](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/acr.22896?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)</u>  
  
- **Primary care:** the Janssens diagnostic rule does not include tophus, and its validation and the primary-care test of the 2015 ACR/EULAR criteria report whole-rule accuracy only.<u>[\[9\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33798500)[\[10\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25231179)[\[11\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28431109)</u>  
  
- **Target mismatch:** a tophus bears on gout only, and the SUGAR comparators were crystal-negative mimics. It therefore does not measure accuracy for "inflammatory or crystal arthritis" as a combined target.[<u>\[7\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1007/s11926-016-0594-8)  
  
- **Elbow:** the olecranon bursa and extensor surface of the elbow are typical tophus sites, but this is descriptive, not accuracy data.<u>[\[12\]](https://doi.org/10.7326/annals-24-03951)[\[13\]](https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMcp2203385?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)</u>

**COUD‑P31: pain aggravated by neck movements (LR+ 5, LR− 0.5 unsupported)**

\- The best interview-item study (Sleijser-Koehorst, Physiotherapy 2021, PMID 33309074; 134 patients, specialist diagnosis plus MRI) found no history item with LR+ ≥5 or LR− ≤0.20. The items it reports as useful do not include neck-movement provocation.[<u>\[3\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33309074)  
  
- A systematic review of self-report items (Mizer, Musculoskelet Sci Pract 2017) concluded that individual history questions have minimal diagnostic value.[<u>\[4\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28644963)  
  
- Wainner (Spine 2003, PMID 12544957) assessed self-report items against electromyography, but item-level values were not retrievable.[<u>\[14\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12544957)  
  
- Narrative reviews describe radicular pain worsening with rotation or bending toward the symptomatic side, without accuracy figures. Mechanical neck pain is also often worsened by neck motion, which argues against high specificity.<u>[\[15\]](https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMcp043887?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)[\[16\]](https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMra2003558?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)</u>

**COUD‑P32: Spurling (LR+ 5 plausible, LR− 0.4 too optimistic)**

\- **LR+:** 5 sits inside Tong's interval, and Tong's definition of a positive test (radiation to the elbow) is the closest match to this questionnaire.[<u>\[6\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11805661)  
  
- **LR−:** 0.4 is lower than Tong's 0.75, whose interval reaches 1.0. A negative Spurling test should not be credited with meaningful rule-out value on this evidence.[<u>\[6\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11805661)  
  
- **Higher published values:** sensitivity 0.92–0.95 and specificity 0.94–0.95 come from surgical or imaging-selected series (Shah, Br J Neurosurg 2004; Shabat, J Neuroimaging 2012).<u>[\[17\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15799149)[\[18\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21883627)</u>  
  
- **Consistency:** Sleijser-Koehorst found high specificity for Spurling but no LR+ ≥5 or LR− ≤0.20.[<u>\[3\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33309074)  
  
- **Composite item:** no study evaluated "Spurling or neck movements reproducing the pain" as a single finding.

**Leads to verify before entering any number**

These two figures are recalled from the literature, not retrieved in this search, and are deliberately left out of the table.  
  
- **Wainner 2003, Spurling A:** widely quoted as sensitivity 0.50, specificity 0.86, LR+ 3.5 (1.6–7.5), LR− 0.58 (0.36–0.94). The abstract only confirms wide intervals for all items.[<u>\[14\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12544957)  
  
- **EULAR 2006 diagnostic recommendations (Zhang, Ann Rheum Dis):** recalled as giving a per-feature LR+ for definite tophus of about 40, derived from older rheumatology datasets. If confirmed, it would replace the provisional 8, with a spectrum caveat.  
  
Would you like the same verification applied to the questionnaire's other cervical radiculopathy items, such as paraesthesia, arm pain worse than neck pain, or the upper limb neurodynamic tests, for which pooled estimates exist?

Whether any of these items has a published 2×2 against a reference standard is the deciding factor: location-of-pain and inspection findings are rarely studied as index tests, unlike named provocation tests. Let me check the elbow diagnostic-accuracy literature by diagnosis group.  
  
  
  
**None of the 25 provisional LR pairs can be confirmed from published accuracy data.** For 23 codes no study reports sensitivity, specificity or LRs for the finding against the stated target; the two exceptions (P9, P14) have data only for a proxy target. Two systematic reviews of elbow examination found no eligible accuracy studies for medial epicondylitis, triceps rupture or posteromedial impingement, and no palpation or pain-location accuracy for lateral epicondylitis.<u>[\[1\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28249855)[\[2\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33814224)</u>

**The two codes with proxy data**

**COUD-P9.** The Sakoda figures are arithmetic from the abstract's counts (204/409 throwing vs 1/97 non-throwing elbows).[<u>\[4\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42200577) The abstract does not define "throwing elbow" precisely, and the target is any radiographic osseous abnormality, not apophysitis. With those limits, the proxy points to an LR+ near 1.5 and an LR− far below 1, which is the opposite pattern to the provisional 5 and 1.  
  
**COUD-P14.** The provisional 1.5 and 0.1 match the direction of Appelboam's fracture data, but no study reports dislocation-specific accuracy.[<u>\[11\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19066257) Later emergency cohorts were less favorable for fracture:  
  
- **Jie 2014** (Ann Emerg Med, PMID 24530106): 88% sensitivity and 55% specificity, giving a derived LR− of 0.22†.[<u>\[25\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24530106)  
  
- **Arundel 2014** (Emerg Med J, PMID 23397014): 84% and 54%, giving a derived LR− of 0.30†.[<u>\[26\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23397014)  
  
No pooled meta-analysis of the extension test was found.

**Nearest evidence for the "no data" codes**

These are different constructs from the coded findings and should not be substituted for them.  
  
- **P6 and P28:** In Blonna (Am J Sports Med 2025; 189 patients labelled lateral epicondylitis at a referral center), maximal tenderness away from the anterolateral epicondyle had 90% sensitivity and 99% specificity for a non-epicondylitis diagnosis. Tenderness 3–4 cm distal had 29% sensitivity and 88% specificity for any alternative diagnosis, with only 3 nerve-compression cases.[<u>\[27\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1177/03635465251319545)  
  
- **P17 and P18:** In a compilation of case series (Reilly, J Shoulder Elbow Surg 2016, PMID 26577126), septic versus aseptic frequencies were tenderness 88% vs 36%, erythema 83% vs 27%, warmth 84% vs 56%, and fever 38% vs 0%.[<u>\[28\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26577126)  
  
- **P19 and P20:** The moving valgus stress test had 100% sensitivity (17/17) and 75% specificity (3/4) in O'Driscoll's surgical series. In the 2026 Sciascia registry it had 98% sensitivity, 75% specificity, LR+ 3.94 and LR− 0.02.<u>[\[14\]](https://doi.org/10.1177/0363546504267804)[\[15\]](https://doi.org/10.1177/03635465261439107)</u>  
  
- **P22:** Radial tuberosity footprint tenderness had 90.5% sensitivity, 11.4% specificity, LR+ 1.02 and LR− 0.84 for complete rupture among 86 patients with anterior elbow complaints.[<u>\[18\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1177/03635465221129874)  
  
- **P24:** In Kida (Am J Sports Med 2014; 2,433 adolescent players, ultrasound reference), current throwing pain was present in 27/82 with [<u>osteochondritis dissecans</u>](https://www.openevidence.com/rare-disease/osteochondritis-dissecans) and 388/2351 without. That gives a derived LR+ of 2.00† and LR− of 0.80† for pain, not locking.[<u>\[20\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1177/0363546514536843)  
  
- **P29:** In ten Brinck (BMJ Open 2019, PMID 30798292), a low painful joint count (1–3) was associated with inflammatory arthritis, so more painful joints did not raise probability. In the SUGAR study, a polyarticular pattern occurred in 8.6% of gout and 5.1% of non-gout.<u>[\[29\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30798292)[\[30\]](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/acr.22585?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)</u>

**Structural issues with the provisional values**

These points are reasoning about the questionnaire design, not literature findings.  
  
- **Definitional findings (P12, P13, P15–P18):** Deformity and bursal swelling are how the diagnosis is recognized, so an accuracy study would have incorporation bias; none appears to exist.  
  
- **Identical LRs for septic and aseptic bursitis:** The same swelling item cannot carry a high LR+ for both, because it identifies bursitis but not its cause.  
  
- **One finding, three targets:** Medial epicondyle pain or tenderness is assigned LR+ 5–6 for medial epicondylitis, apophysitis and ligament injury. These can only coexist if each is conditioned on age and activity, which the item does not encode.  
  
Would it be useful to extract the full-text 2×2 tables from the Japanese youth baseball checkup studies (Harada, Kurokawa, Otoshi, Matsuura) to see whether medial tenderness accuracy for P10 and P11 can be calculated?

Whether any of these findings has published accuracy data depends on the few primary diagnostic studies behind the Cozen and Maudsley tests and on whether radial tunnel tenderness has ever been tested against a reference standard; let me check the systematic reviews and primary studies for each.  
  
  
  
Two points remain open: the primary study behind the Cozen and Maudsley sensitivities, and the 2×2 data for locking and radial-tunnel tenderness in the two orthopaedic cohorts.  
  
  
  
**None of the four current LR pairs can be confirmed from a published accuracy study.** The direct literature gives sensitivity only for Cozen and Maudsley, and nothing for the other two items. The only LR-capable figures come from inverting one orthopaedic referral cohort, and no primary-care or sports-medicine accuracy study was found for any item.

†Calculated here by inverting the published sensitivity and specificity in Blonna's Table 2, which reports each finding's accuracy for "not lateral epicondylitis"; the authors do not report these LRs.[<u>\[1\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1177/03635465251319545)

**Verdict on the current values**

\- **COUD-P1 (0.4 / 1.05): not supported; set to "no data".** The Blonna variable is examiner-observed mechanical symptoms during motion (clicking, snapping, crepitus or locking), not a history item. Absence of mechanical symptoms was part of the definition of the typical epicondylitis group, which inflates the result. The 99% specificity among 168 epicondylitis patients rests on only 1–2 patients, so the ≈0.04 is unstable. A second cohort of 170 consecutive orthopaedic outpatients found joint locking independently predicted a diagnosis other than tendinopathy (P \< .001), but the abstract gives no accuracy figures.<u>[\[1\]](https://doi.org/10.1177/03635465251319545)[\[7\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41192654)</u>  
  
- **COUD-P2 (2.5 / 0.12): LR+ matches the indirect figure only; LR− is untraceable.** The 91% sensitivity comes from a review in which only 2 studies assessed physical tests and 97% of all studies were at unclear or high risk of bias. A positive Cozen test was a criterion for the typical epicondylitis group in Blonna, so the indirect LRs are subject to incorporation bias.<u>[\[2\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33814224)[\[1\]](https://doi.org/10.1177/03635465251319545)</u>  
  
- **COUD-P3 (2 / 0.4): not supported; set to "no data" for both LRs.** Only sensitivity is published, and the primary study behind it was not identified. A negative Maudsley test independently predicted a non-tendinopathy diagnosis in the 170-patient cohort (P \< .001), without accuracy figures in the abstract.<u>[\[3\]](https://www.aafp.org/pubs/afp/issues/2026/1100/p469.html)[\[7\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41192654)</u>  
  
- **COUD-P4 (5 / 0.3): not supported; set to "no data".** Radial tunnel syndrome is a clinical diagnosis of exclusion with typically normal electrodiagnostic and MRI findings, so no accepted reference standard exists. One study used an ultrasound-guided posterior interosseous nerve block as the reference in 32 patients with refractory epicondylitis, but its abstract reports no accuracy for palpation tenderness. Blonna's "PIN pain" figures (sensitivity 29%, specificity 88%) target "not epicondylitis", and only 3 of 21 such patients had nerve compression, so they cannot be used for this item.<u>[\[5\]](https://doi.org/10.1177/1753193420953990)[\[3\]](https://www.aafp.org/pubs/afp/issues/2026/1100/p469.html)[\[6\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41082715)[\[1\]](https://doi.org/10.1177/03635465251319545)</u>

**Gaps that full texts could close**

\- **Karanasios 2022 evidence tables:** these would show whether the two primary physical-test studies reported any specificity for Cozen or Maudsley; neither primary study could be retrieved here.[<u>\[2\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33814224)  
  
- **Zwerus 2026 full text:** this may contain 2×2 data for locking and for Maudsley in consecutive patients, which would be the least biased source for COUD-P1 and COUD-P3.[<u>\[7\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41192654)  
  
- **Zwerus 2018 systematic review:** it included only one lateral epicondylitis study and concluded that no elbow test provides adequate certainty to rule a diagnosis in or out.[<u>\[8\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28249855)  
  
Would it help to look at grip-strength difference between elbow flexion and extension, the only lateral epicondylitis finding with both sensitivity and specificity published, as a candidate replacement item?

†Calculated here by inverting the published sensitivity and specificity in Blonna's Table 2, which reports each finding's accuracy for "not lateral epicondylitis"; the authors do not report these LRs.[<u>\[1\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1177/03635465251319545)

**Verdict on the current values**

\- **COUD-P1 (0.4 / 1.05): not supported; set to "no data".** The Blonna variable is examiner-observed mechanical symptoms during motion (clicking, snapping, crepitus or locking), not a history item. Absence of mechanical symptoms was part of the definition of the typical epicondylitis group, which inflates the result. The 99% specificity among 168 epicondylitis patients rests on only 1–2 patients, so the ≈0.04 is unstable. A second cohort of 170 consecutive orthopaedic outpatients found joint locking independently predicted a diagnosis other than tendinopathy (P \< .001), but the abstract gives no accuracy figures.<u>[\[1\]](https://doi.org/10.1177/03635465251319545)[\[7\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41192654)</u>  
  
- **COUD-P2 (2.5 / 0.12): LR+ matches the indirect figure only; LR− is untraceable.** The 91% sensitivity comes from a review in which only 2 studies assessed physical tests and 97% of all studies were at unclear or high risk of bias. A positive Cozen test was a criterion for the typical epicondylitis group in Blonna, so the indirect LRs are subject to incorporation bias.<u>[\[2\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33814224)[\[1\]](https://doi.org/10.1177/03635465251319545)</u>  
  
- **COUD-P3 (2 / 0.4): not supported; set to "no data" for both LRs.** Only sensitivity is published, and the primary study behind it was not identified. A negative Maudsley test independently predicted a non-tendinopathy diagnosis in the 170-patient cohort (P \< .001), without accuracy figures in the abstract.<u>[\[3\]](https://www.aafp.org/pubs/afp/issues/2026/1100/p469.html)[\[7\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41192654)</u>  
  
- **COUD-P4 (5 / 0.3): not supported; set to "no data".** Radial tunnel syndrome is a clinical diagnosis of exclusion with typically normal electrodiagnostic and MRI findings, so no accepted reference standard exists. One study used an ultrasound-guided posterior interosseous nerve block as the reference in 32 patients with refractory epicondylitis, but its abstract reports no accuracy for palpation tenderness. Blonna's "PIN pain" figures (sensitivity 29%, specificity 88%) target "not epicondylitis", and only 3 of 21 such patients had nerve compression, so they cannot be used for this item.<u>[\[5\]](https://doi.org/10.1177/1753193420953990)[\[3\]](https://www.aafp.org/pubs/afp/issues/2026/1100/p469.html)[\[6\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41082715)[\[1\]](https://doi.org/10.1177/03635465251319545)</u>

**Gaps that full texts could close**

\- **Karanasios 2022 evidence tables:** these would show whether the two primary physical-test studies reported any specificity for Cozen or Maudsley; neither primary study could be retrieved here.[<u>\[2\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33814224)  
  
- **Zwerus 2026 full text:** this may contain 2×2 data for locking and for Maudsley in consecutive patients, which would be the least biased source for COUD-P1 and COUD-P3.[<u>\[7\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41192654)  
  
- **Zwerus 2018 systematic review:** it included only one lateral epicondylitis study and concluded that no elbow test provides adequate certainty to rule a diagnosis in or out.[<u>\[8\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28249855)  
  
Would it help to look at grip-strength difference between elbow flexion and extension, the only lateral epicondylitis finding with both sensitivity and specificity published, as a candidate replacement item?

The answer hinges on whether any primary-care or sports-medicine cohort reports the diagnostic case-mix of elbow-pain consulters by age, as opposed to population incidence figures for each condition separately; let me check the epidemiology for each of the 17 diagnoses.  
  
  
  
**None of the 17 estimates can be confirmed or corrected in absolute terms.** No study retrieved reports the diagnostic case-mix of patients consulting primary care or sports medicine for elbow pain, by age, for any of these codes. The closest outpatient statement is that lateral tendinopathy makes up more than half of specific elbow diagnoses in adults, followed by medial tendinopathy, with no age split.[<u>\[1\]</u>](https://www.aafp.org/pubs/afp/issues/2026/1100/p469.html)  
  
In the table, the proportion column reads "no data" throughout. The figure that follows in each cell is the nearest published one, with its real denominator, and is not a substitute for the proportion. PMIDs are given only where retrieved; "n/r" means not retrieved.

**What the published data support or contradict**

Only relative features can be checked, not magnitudes.  
  
**Consistent with the published data**  
  
- **F1 age shape:** incidence peaks at 40–49 years and is low under 30 and over 70.[<u>\[3\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1177/0363546514568087)  
  
- **F2 relative to F1:** the estimates put medial at roughly a quarter of lateral, which matches the medial-to-lateral ratio in the Finnish, US military and Korean data.<u>[\[4\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16968862)[\[5\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20486505)[\[6\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40020113)</u>  
  
- **F3 age decline:** medial epicondyle abnormalities fall sharply once the physis closes.[<u>\[9\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42200577)  
  
- **F5 adolescent peak:** dislocation incidence is highest at 10–19 years.[<u>\[15\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22298056)  
  
- **F6 age peak:** incidence peaks at 55–64 years in UK primary care.[<u>\[16\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16421136)  
  
- **F8 relative to F7:** the estimates make septic about one third of olecranon bursitis, in line with 24% to one third in the literature.<u>[\[20\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28885965)[\[23\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37995861)</u>  
  
- **F9 sex and age direction:** male predominance and a rise with age are both reported.<u>[\[24\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22958843)[\[26\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34454037)</u>  
  
**Points where the data argue for a change**  
  
- **F11 sex multipliers:** ×10 and ×0.1 imply 100:1, whereas series show 95–96% male (about 19–24:1). On a both-sex average with an even sex mix, 95% male corresponds to about ×1.9 and ×0.1.[<u>\[31\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1177/0363546515587738)  
  
- **F14 sex multipliers:** ×2 and ×0.6 imply about 3:1, whereas the largest rupture series is 93% male.[<u>\[35\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1177/0363546518757426)  
  
- **F6 sex multipliers:** ×1.4 and ×0.75 imply 1.9:1, the top of the published range; UK primary care gives 1.3:1.[<u>\[16\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16421136)  
  
- **F10 sex and peak age:** no sex term is set, yet reconstruction is 88% male. Reconstruction incidence peaks at 15–19 years, not 22, although the New York mean age was 21.6.<u>[\[28\]](https://doi.org/10.1177/0363546515580304)[\[29\]](https://doi.org/10.1177/0363546515622407)</u>  
  
- **F12 sex:** no sex term is set, yet incidence is 3.8 versus 0.6 per 100,000 in males versus females.[<u>\[33\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1007/s12178-020-09606-2)  
  
- **F4 and F5 sex by age:** women predominate in radial head and olecranon fractures after 50, and boys have about twice the dislocation rate of girls at 10–19 years.<u>[\[11\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20149972)[\[13\]](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00068-021-01765-2)[\[14\]](https://doi.org/10.1177/0363546515610527)</u>  
  
**Not checkable at all:** every absolute percentage, the F3 sex multipliers, and all of F13, F16 and F17.

**Community age pattern**

The closest view of case-mix by age is a UK community survey of adults aged 25–64, where nonspecific elbow pain outnumbered any epicondylitis (89 versus 28 men; 90 versus 42 women).[<u>\[41\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1002/art.20535?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral) Its Figure 2 shows prevalence by age and sex for lateral epicondylitis, medial epicondylitis and nonspecific elbow pain; this is population prevalence, not consulters.  
  
<img src="media/image8.jpeg" style="width:7.23681in;height:8.46806in" alt="https://storage.googleapis.com/openevidence-multimedia/wiley_research/cms/10.1002_art.20535/asset/8fc705e3-79c8-58ae-b5bd-47b119500cce/assets/images/large/nfig002.jpg" />Figure 2 The estimated prevalence in the general population of specific disorders and nonspecific pain by age and sex at the shoulder, elbow, and wrist or hand.

**Gap**

A primary-care or sports-clinic database reporting coded elbow diagnoses by age and sex among all elbow-pain visits would settle these estimates. The Dutch general-practice elbow cohort (Bot, Ann Rheum Dis 2005, PMID 15708885; n=181) is the most likely existing source, but its diagnostic breakdown was not retrievable.[<u>\[42\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15708885)  
  
Would you like a focused look at the adolescent thrower subset (F3, F10, F12, F13), where symptomatic case series give the most usable diagnostic proportions?

Each row depends on whether a published study reports the finding against a reference standard for that exact target diagnosis, so the evidence splits into four areas: frozen shoulder, acromioclavicular/clavicle/instability trauma findings, polymyalgia rheumatica, and cervical radiculopathy with biceps rupture. Let me check the diagnostic accuracy literature for each.  
  
  
  
**Only 2 of the 21 codes (EPAU-P27 in part, EPAU-P28) have published accuracy for the finding as worded against the stated target diagnosis.** For the other 19, no accuracy study was identified, so the provisional likelihood ratios can be neither confirmed nor corrected from the literature. "No data" means none was found in the retrievable text, not that none exists.

| EPAU-P28 | Deltoid weakness 0.24 (0.03–0.44); biceps weakness 0.24 (0.03–0.44); reduced biceps reflex 0.24 (0.03–0.44); reduced brachioradialis reflex 0.06 (0–0.17); C5 pinprick loss 0.29 (0.08–0.51); C6 pinprick loss 0.24 (0.03–0.44) | Same order: 0.89 (0.81–0.97); 0.94 (0.88–1.00); 0.95 (0.90–1.00); 0.95 (0.90–1.00); 0.86 (0.77–0.94); 0.66 (0.54–0.78) | Same order: 2.1; 3.7; 4.9; 1.2; 2.1; 0.69 | Same order: 0.86; 0.82; 0.80; 0.99; 0.82; 1.16 (CIs not retrieved) | 82 adults referred for electrodiagnosis with suspected cervical radiculopathy or carpal tunnel syndrome; 4 US facilities | Needle EMG plus nerve conduction studies                     | Wainner, Spine, 2003, PMID 12544957                                                                                       | [<u>\[20\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12891-025-08560-9)                                                                                                                                          |

**Closest evidence for the "no data" rows**

These are related findings or different targets, so they are not substitutes for the rows above.  
  
**Adhesive capsulitis (P8–P13)**  
  
- Restricted passive external rotation is part of the case definition, for example as an entry criterion in the UK FROST trial, so its accuracy cannot be tested independently.[<u>\[21\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33010843)  
  
- Pain on passive external rotation with the arm at the side had a positive predictive value of 75% (68/91) in an orthopaedic series; the other positives had glenohumeral arthritis.[<u>\[2\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20506951)  
  
- Range-of-motion criteria had a positive predictive value of 82% against capsular volume in 38 referred patients, with no non-cases to give specificity.[<u>\[22\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31742369)  
  
- External rotation restricted with a hard end-feel is listed as a rule-out criterion in one Dutch criteria set, which bears on P13.[<u>\[23\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00296-021-04979-0)  
  
**Acromioclavicular joint and clavicle (P14–P16, P20)**  
  
- Joint tenderness had 96% sensitivity and low specificity for injection-confirmed joint pain in 38 specialist-practice patients (Walton, J Bone Joint Surg Am, 2004, PMID 15069148).[<u>\[6\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15069148)  
  
- In 153 primary care patients, localized joint tenderness was not associated with a positive anaesthetic block (Cadogan, BMC Musculoskelet Disord, 2013, PMID 23634871).[<u>\[5\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23634871)  
  
- Emergency physicians predicted all 94 clavicle fractures before radiography, with 6 false-positive predictions among 12 non-fractures (Landine, CJEM, 2001, PMID 17610797).[<u>\[7\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17610797)  
  
- When physicians were certain of clavicle fracture, radiography confirmed it in 50 of 51 cases (Shuster, CJEM, 2003, PMID 17466138).[<u>\[8\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17466138)  
  
**Anterior dislocation or instability (P17–P19)**  
  
- The JAMA Rational Clinical Examination review found no accuracy studies of history taking for shoulder instability.[<u>\[9\]</u>](https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jama/fullarticle/10.1001/jama.292.16.1989?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)  
  
- Previous dislocation was a predictor in a multivariable model (AUC 0.95) against MR arthrography, without single-item values in the retrieved text (van Kampen, J Shoulder Elbow Surg, 2013, PMID 23850309).[<u>\[10\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23850309)  
  
- The apprehension test, scored on apprehension, had sensitivity 72%, specificity 96% and LR+ 20.2 against arthroscopy (Farber, J Bone Joint Surg Am, 2006, PMID 16818971).[<u>\[24\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16818971)  
  
- Physical examination alone had LR+ 2.03 and LR− 0.35 for dislocation in an emergency department trial (Attard Biancardi, Emerg Med J, 2022, PMID 34544780).[<u>\[12\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34544780)  
  
**Polymyalgia rheumatica (P21–P23)**  
  
The only item-level data come from the 2012 EULAR/ACR derivation cohort (Dasgupta, Arthritis Rheum, 2012; PMID not verified). It compared 125 cases with 169 comparators in rheumatology clinics, all aged 50 or older with new bilateral shoulder pain, using clinician diagnosis at 6 months as reference.[<u>\[14\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1002/art.34356?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral) The likelihood ratios below are calculated from its published proportions, not reported by the authors.  
  
- Bilateral pelvic girdle aching was present in 73% of cases and 53% of comparators, giving LR+ 1.37 and LR− 0.58.[<u>\[14\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1002/art.34356?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)  
  
- Morning stiffness over 45 minutes was present in 77% of cases and 43% of comparators, giving LR+ 1.78 and LR− 0.41.[<u>\[14\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1002/art.34356?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)  
  
- Against other shoulder conditions only, morning stiffness over 45 minutes gave LR+ 3.83 and LR− 0.29; against rheumatoid arthritis, LR+ 1.12.[<u>\[14\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1002/art.34356?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)  
  
These figures sit well below the provisional LR+ of 5 to 10. They cannot be transferred to an unselected shoulder-pain population, because every participant already had bilateral shoulder pain.[<u>\[14\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1002/art.34356?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)  
  
**Cervical spine (P25–P28)**  
  
- All cervical data target radiculopathy, not somatic referred pain.  
  
- A systematic review of history items for painful neck conditions concluded that individual items have minimal diagnostic value (Mizer, Musculoskelet Sci Pract, 2017, PMID 28644963).[<u>\[17\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28644963)  
  
- The Spurling test had sensitivity 0.38–0.98 and specificity 0.84–1.00 across five studies (Thoomes, BMC Musculoskelet Disord, 2026, PMID 41680685).[<u>\[18\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41680685)  
  
- For P27, the published direction is high sensitivity without a useful LR+, which does not support the provisional LR+ of 5.[<u>\[19\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33309074)  
  
- For P28, the provisional LR+ 5 and LR− 0.8 match the best single component (biceps reflex, LR+ 4.9, LR− 0.80), but other components range from LR+ 0.69 to 3.7.[<u>\[20\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12891-025-08560-9)  
  
- Any abnormal neurological sign had sensitivity 0.84 and specificity 0.44 against EMG (Lauder, Arch Phys Med Rehabil, 2000, PMID 10768532).[<u>\[25\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10768532)

**Papers whose full-text tables should be checked**

The item-level tables of these papers could not be read and may fill some rows:  
  
- **van Kampen 2013:** history items (P17, P18).  
  
- **Wainner 2003:** self-report items (P25, P26).  
  
- **Sleijser-Koehorst 2021:** exact values for P27.  
  
- **Cadogan 2013 and Walton 2004:** individual tenderness values (P15, P20).  
  
- **Bird, Ann Rheum Dis, 1979, PMID 518143:** possible per-item values (P21–P23).  
  
Would you like the published accuracy of the named provocative tests (apprehension/relocation, Spurling, arm squeeze, cross-body adduction, Yergason) assembled in the same format, as possible replacements for the unvalidated items?

Each row depends on whether a published study reports that exact finding against that exact target condition, so the Neer meta-analyses, the Park 2005 clusters, the diabetes–frozen shoulder data, scapular dyskinesis test validity, and the acromioclavicular pain studies need to be checked separately; let me pull those sources.  
  
  
  
**Only EPAU-P1 can be corrected with pooled figures. For the other six codes, the published record I could retrieve either has no accuracy data or does not show the item-level numbers.** In the table, "not retrieved" means a study exists but its figure was not in the accessible text. "No data" means no accuracy study was found.

**Verdict by code**

**EPAU-P1: correct both values.** The current LR− of 0.8 is too weak for this target; the pooled estimate is 0.47. A meta-analysis restricted to two low-risk-of-bias studies (Gismervik 2017) gave LR+ 1.48 and LR− 0.68. The current 1.3/0.8 pair resembles Neer for rotator cuff tear (LR+ 1.40, LR− 0.78) rather than subacromial impingement. Studies in the earlier reviews were not done in primary care.<u>[\[1\]](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12891-024-08144-z)[\[9\]](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12891-017-1400-0)[\[10\]](https://doi.org/10.7326/0003-4819-160-6-201403180-02012)</u>  
  
**EPAU-P2 and EPAU-P4: post-test probabilities confirmed, LR+ values not.** The abstract confirms 95% and 91% but gives no likelihood ratios or cluster sensitivity and specificity. The 10.6 and 15.6 figures match what is usually quoted from the full-text tables, but I could not check them against a source.[<u>\[2\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15995110) Three cautions apply:  
  
- **Cohort dependence:** The post-test probabilities come from a surgical cohort, so they do not transfer to primary care.[<u>\[3\]</u>](https://www.cochranelibrary.com/cdsr/doi/10.1002/14651858.CD007427.pub2/full?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)  
  
- **Study quality:** The Cochrane review left the study "awaiting classification" because of retrospective collection over 11 years and inconsistent test application.[<u>\[3\]</u>](https://www.cochranelibrary.com/cdsr/doi/10.1002/14651858.CD007427.pub2/full?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)  
  
- **Conflicting secondary citation:** One review attributes LR+ 16.35 (5.9–43.1) for full-thickness tear to Hawkins + painful arc + resisted external rotation, which mixes the two clusters.[<u>\[11\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.7326/aitc201501060)  
  
**EPAU-P3: no data; 2.5/0.85 has no traceable source.** A systematic review of clinical tests for adhesive capsulitis identified only physical examination tests and no validated set. Diabetes has association data only: pooled odds ratio 3.69 (2.99–4.56) against people without diabetes, not against other shoulder-pain patients. Nothing was found for peripheral neuropathy.<u>[\[4\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37737049)[\[5\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36599641)</u>  
  
**EPAU-P5: 3/0.6 not confirmed.** The finding and the target are the same construct, so accuracy only exists against 3D kinematics. Uhl 2009 reports sensitivity 76% and positive predictive value 74%. A later paper cites kinematic asymmetry in 71–77% of both symptomatic and asymptomatic participants, which is hard to reconcile with an LR+ of 3. Tate 2009 (overhead athletes, sports medicine) gives group differences only, no sensitivity or specificity.<u>[\[6\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19896045)[\[12\]](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-017-06779-8)[\[13\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19295961)</u>  
  
**EPAU-P6: direction supported, magnitude not confirmed.** In Cadogan 2013, absence of referred pain below the elbow was associated with injection-confirmed acromioclavicular pain. That fits an LR+ below 1 for pain that does radiate below the elbow, but the item-level values are not in the abstract.[<u>\[7\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23634871)  
  
**EPAU-P7: LR− 0.1 is probably misattributed.** I read « Acromio-claviculaire » as either pain located at the joint or tenderness on palpation.  
  
- **Tenderness:** In primary care, localised joint tenderness was not associated with a positive block, and traditional tests combined did no better than chance (AUC 0.507).[<u>\[7\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23634871)  
  
- **Likely origin of 0.1:** Cadogan's LR− of 0.09 (0.02–0.41) belongs to "fewer than two of five features", not to tenderness or pain location.[<u>\[7\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23634871)  
  
- **Pain location:** No data. Walton used it as an entry criterion, and Chronopoulos used it as part of the reference standard.<u>[\[8\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15069148)[\[14\]](https://doi.org/10.1177/0363546503261723)</u>

**What would settle the open cells**

The missing figures should be in the full-text tables of Park 2005 (combination table), Cadogan 2013 (item-level table), Walton 2004 (tenderness specificity) and Uhl 2009 (specificity of the yes/no method).  
  
Would you like the same check run on the single-test components of the Park clusters (painful arc, drop-arm, resisted external rotation), for which pooled imaging-referenced estimates exist?

The table depends on finding cohorts that report diagnostic case-mix among patients actually consulting for shoulder pain, as opposed to population incidence, and on whether any stratify by age and sex. I'll look at the primary care diagnostic-distribution cohorts, the imaging-based series, and the trauma and sports medicine data.  
  
  
  
**None of the 17 age curves can be confirmed as written.** No retrieved study reports diagnostic case-mix among shoulder-pain consulters in primary care or sports medicine by the bands 12–17, 18–39, 40–59 and 60+, and none includes adolescents. What exists is two unstratified adult general practice (GP) cohorts, one age- and sex-stratified secondary-care cohort, and GP-referred ultrasound series. For the trauma codes there are only injury-denominator or population-incidence data.  
  
In the table, "bands" means the four requested age groups. Sex ratios marked "derived" are simple divisions of published counts or rates. "Multipliers imply" is the ratio of your two sex multipliers.

The only age- and sex-stratified diagnostic distribution found is the Juel and Natvig secondary-care cohort, whose Figure 1 plots the six main diagnoses by age group for women and men.  
  
<img src="media/image20.jpeg" style="width:12.50278in;height:17.11528in" alt="https://storage.googleapis.com/openevidence-multimedia/springer/cms/10.1186_1471-2474-15-89/asset/83bdbf6e-aa96-530e-8b20-6301c88766dc/assets/images/large/12891_2013_Article_2077_Fig1_HTML.jpg" />Figure 1 Percentages of the six most common shoulder diagnoses among six age groups in women (red squares, dotted line) and men (blue diamonds).

**Limits on using these figures**

\- **Denominators differ from yours.** The Dutch GP cohort enrolled only intrinsic shoulder disorders, the UK GP cohort allowed several diagnoses per patient, and the ultrasound series counted imaging findings in patients a GP chose to refer.<u>[\[1\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8546527)[\[2\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15769790)[\[5\]](https://doi.org/10.1186/s13075-017-1235-y)</u>  
  
- **Trauma codes (F4, F7, F8, F11, F17)** have only injury-denominator data. In the Oslo cohort, fractures were 35% and dislocations 17% of all acute shoulder injuries.[<u>\[23\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29861311)  
  
- **Ages 12–17:** only school athletic-trainer surveillance exists, with broad categories (sprain/strain 39.6%, dislocation/separation 23.7%, fracture 6.6% of shoulder injuries).[<u>\[42\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19180222)  
  
- **Juel's age bands** are under 30 and then decades to 70 and over, so they do not map onto the requested groups.[<u>\[3\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1186/1471-2474-15-89)  
  
- **PMIDs** are given only where the retrieved record showed one. Full-text tables of the two GP cohorts were not examined and may hold more age detail.  
  
Would it help to look at how these case-mix estimates shift if the denominator is restricted to non-traumatic presentations, which is how most primary care cohorts were defined?

Each row depends on whether a published accuracy study exists for that exact finding against that exact diagnosis, so I'll check the primary diagnostic-accuracy literature and meta-analyses for the headache and radicular items, the sports, torticollis and polymyalgia items, and the myelopathy, rheumatoid, infection and occipital neuralgia items.  
  
  
  
**Only CERV-P22 (Babinski) has a directly published accuracy estimate; for the other 11 codes no study was found that reports sensitivity, specificity or likelihood ratios for the coded finding against the stated diagnosis.** Those 11 current values can be neither confirmed nor corrected from published data. Item-level tables in a few full texts could not be read (listed at the end).

**Verdict on CERV-P22**

The current LR+ 5 / LR− 0.9 is close to the published range. Cook 2009 gives LR+ 4.0 and LR− 0.7, with a wide interval on the LR+.[<u>\[14\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19252263) Two other studies give low sensitivity and very high specificity, with LRs calculated here from their reported figures (no CIs):  
  
- **Rhee 2009 (Spine, PMID 19352222):** 39 surgical myelopathy patients versus 37 neck or radicular controls. Sensitivity 13% and specificity 100% are attributed to "sustained clonus or Babinski" in a secondary source, giving LR− 0.87 and an incalculable LR+.<u>[\[22\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19352222)[\[23\]](https://www.aafp.org/pubs/afp/issues/2020/1215/p740.html)</u>  
  
- **Soufi 2025 (Sci Rep):** 139 myelopathy patients versus 108 healthy controls, sensitivity 12%, specificity 98%, giving LR+ 6.0 and LR− 0.90. Healthy controls inflate specificity relative to a neck-pain population.[<u>\[24\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-025-20928-4)  
  
A 2025 scoping review of case-control studies reports Babinski specificity of 93–100% without pooling.[<u>\[25\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40011743) No pooled meta-analytic estimate was retrieved, and no primary-care study was found.

**Why the other codes have no data**

\- **CERV-P14:** A 2022 systematic review of cervicogenic headache diagnosis could meta-analyse only the cervical flexion-rotation test (LR+ 5.0, 95% CI 2.6–9.5; LR− 0.2, 0.1–0.4). A 2025 systematic review found no data supporting the clinical features described for cervicogenic headache.<u>[\[1\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36088782)[\[2\]](https://doi.org/10.1177/03331024251322446)</u>  
  
- **CERV-P15:** Three studies tested history items (Wainner 2003, Lauder 2000, Sleijser-Koehorst 2021), but values for "pain below the elbow" or dermatomal arm pain were not retrievable. In Sleijser-Koehorst 2021, no interview item reached LR+ ≥5 or LR− ≤0.20, which argues against the current 6 / 0.2. A systematic review of history items concluded that individual questions have minimal diagnostic value for neck conditions.<u>[\[4\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12544957)[\[26\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10768532)[\[5\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33309074)[\[6\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28644963)</u>  
  
- **CERV-P16:** The coded finding is itself the clinical definition of a stinger, and no reference standard exists for transient cases.<u>[\[7\]](https://doi.org/10.1002/mus.28298?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)[\[8\]](https://doi.org/10.1177/1941738115610753)</u>  
  
- **CERV-P17 and P18:** Only uncontrolled case series and one pediatric emergency prognostic cohort were found; none reports accuracy for posture or rotation.<u>[\[9\]](https://doi.org/10.1007/s10143-024-02315-1)[\[10\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29298248)</u>  
  
- **CERV-P19 to P21:** The 2012 EULAR/ACR study reports adjusted odds ratios, not likelihood ratios: morning stiffness \>45 minutes OR 6.2 (95% CI 3.2–11.8), hip pain or limited motion OR 2.1 (1.1–4.0). Bilateral shoulder pain was an entry requirement, so its accuracy cannot be estimated, and the threshold studied was 45 minutes, not 30.[<u>\[12\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1002/art.34356?utm_source=openevidence&utm_medium=referral)  
  
- **CERV-P23:** Only prevalence data exist, for example atlantoaxial subluxation in 17.9% of 1,114 rheumatoid arthritis patients. An LR− of 0.1 is hard to reconcile with atlantoaxial subluxation in 12.5% of imaged older adults without rheumatoid arthritis.<u>[\[15\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32613395)[\[16\]](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12877-026-08116-z)</u>  
  
- **CERV-P24:** Davis 2004 (emergency, epidural abscess versus spine-pain controls) reports only "any risk factor" in its abstract: 98% of cases versus 21% of controls. The nearest single-item figure has a different target: in 1,000 emergency back-pain patients, injection drug use had sensitivity 12.1%, specificity 98.2%, LR+ 6.9 (2.5–19.4) and LR− 0.9 (0.8–1.0) for any serious spinal pathology. In a prospective emergency cohort, 13.1% of epidural abscess patients used injection drugs.<u>[\[17\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15028325)[\[18\]](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00586-020-06452-1)[\[19\]](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31128933)</u>  
  
- **CERV-P25:** Tenderness over the nerve is part of the ICHD-3 definition of occipital neuralgia, so any accuracy estimate against those criteria is circular. Examination findings specific to occipital neuralgia are not clearly defined.<u>[\[20\]](https://doi.org/10.1007/s11910-019-0937-8)[\[21\]](https://doi.org/10.1007/s11916-024-01288-6)</u>

**Full texts worth pulling**

\- **Wainner 2003 (Spine, PMID 12544957):** history-item table for symptom location.[<u>\[4\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12544957)  
  
- **Cook 2009:** Babinski sensitivity, specificity and sample size.[<u>\[14\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19252263)  
  
- **Dasgupta 2012 (Ann Rheum Dis, PMID 22388996):** per-item frequencies in cases and comparators.[<u>\[27\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22388996)  
  
- **Davis 2004 (J Emerg Med):** injection drug use proportions in cases and controls.[<u>\[17\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15028325)  
  
Would you like the same verification run on combined findings, such as the Wainner four-item cluster for radiculopathy or the 2012 EULAR/ACR score for polymyalgia rheumatica, where published accuracy data do exist?

Each value depends on its originating study's reference standard (electrodiagnosis versus MRI versus clinical consensus) and on whether a meta-analysis or primary-care cohort has since replaced it. Let me check the radiculopathy, myelopathy and thoracic outlet accuracy data in parallel.  
  
  
  
The exact Wainner point estimates and the arm-dominant-pain figures were not in the retrieved text; let me try once more through guideline and review sources that reproduce them.  
  
  
  
**Of the 13 values, five match their source, five need correction, one could not be verified, and two have no published accuracy data.**  
  
- **Match their source:** P2, P6, P7, P8, P9.  
  
- **Need correction:** P3, P4, P5, P10, P11.  
  
- **Not verified:** P1.  
  
- **No data:** P12, P13.  
  
Every study comes from secondary or tertiary care; the 2026 systematic review found none in primary care, and no sports-medicine study was retrieved.[<u>\[1\]</u>](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12891-026-09551-0)  
  
Values marked ‡ are the figures widely reproduced from Wainner 2003. The full-text table was not retrieved, so they are unverified here; the abstract states that all likelihood-ratio confidence intervals were wide.[<u>\[2\]</u>](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12544957)
